a:T42e,The method is a parallel agentic search loop. Human scientists provide a high-level prompt asking Claude to search DNA sequence databases for interesting reverse transcriptase examples. Claude reads the relevant literature, reproduces established results on public data to check its methods, searches protein families and genomic neighborhoods for systems that do not match known categories, and writes short reports for candidates with proposed functions and supporting evidence. Follow-up Claude sessions critique the evidence and eliminate many weak candidates. Human scientists then review the survivors and test them in the lab. The important design choice is not asking the model for a direct answer. The workflow decomposes discovery into literature calibration, database search, anomaly detection, candidate reporting, self-critique, expert filtering, and biochemical follow-up. The team also studies the model’s generated hypotheses as data, comparing candidates that experts keep against those they discard, then feeding those lessons back into instructions.b:T450,Terminal Shrinkage Averaging returns an interpolation between the last checkpoint and the mean of the last K checkpoints: (1-α)θ_T + α\bar{θ}_T. α=0 recovers the raw final iterate, and α=1 recovers uniform LAWA. Since the final checkpoint is included in the average, its total weight is 1-α+α/K, while each earlier checkpoint receives α/K. The analysis uses a local quadratic approximation to the loss over the short terminal region. Checkpoints are decomposed into their across-run mean and stochastic residuals, and risk is measured in the Hessian-induced seminorm. Under this model, the expected surrogate risk is quadratic in α. The expression exposes the tradeoff: averaging older checkpoints can move the mean away from the local optimum, but it can also reduce stochastic terminal variation. This gives conditions where the best α lies strictly between raw final iterate and full LAWA. The same framework explains why an averaged estimator can prefer a higher terminal learning-rate floor: the trajectory keeps making progress late in training, while the estimator damps endpoint noise.c:T432,The experiments are designed around the interaction rather than only the main effects. Depth-12 NanoChat runs first show that the best shrinkage coefficient is inside the interval, not at α=0 or α=1. A paired factorial experiment then crosses terminal learning-rate floors of 5%, 10%, and 15% of peak LR with two output estimators, raw final iterate and TSA, using five repetitions. Increasing the floor worsens validation BPB when deploying the raw endpoint, but improves validation BPB when deploying TSA; the effect grows as the floor is raised. A replication with pure AdamW preserves the direction of the interaction, so the result is not tied to the Muon+AdamW optimizer mix used in NanoChat. The higher-floor-plus-TSA recipe is also applied to a depth-22 NanoChat time-to-GPT-2 setting, where it improves over the public recipe at matched budget and yields a qualifying benchmark run. The provided material does not include the exact BPB table, so the reliable quantitative details are the floor values, repetition count, model depths, and direction of the effects.d:T7dd,Hill Sampling is intentionally minimal. Start from an initial program x0. At each round, sample N candidate edits from a frozen LLM conditioned on the current incumbent xt. Execute each candidate with the verifier, obtain rewards, and replace the incumbent if the best candidate in the round matches or exceeds the best reward seen so far. The algorithm stores the incumbent’s historical best reward rather than re-evaluating it every round, which is important when execution is stochastic. Equal-reward replacements are allowed to preserve some structural diversity. There is no archive, no crossover, no explicit diversity objective, and no parameter update. The main knobs are decoding temperature, samples per round, and number of rounds.

The paper is stronger because it does not stop at introducing the method. It systematically compares three exploration families. First, repeated sampling, where all generations are drawn independently from the same starting point. Relative to that baseline, Hill Sampling adds exactly one persistent state: the best-so-far program fed back into context. Second, evolution strategies applied directly to LLM weights. The authors build a large-scale ES pipeline and separate standard ES learning from a zero-learning-rate variant that keeps only random weight perturbations. This isolates exploration from learning. Third, they test common add-ons such as diversity encouragement, solution combination, extra exploration mechanisms, and code-execution feedback. The conclusion is that none of these are necessary to obtain strong results in this setting.

Mechanistically, Hill Sampling treats the LLM as a conditional editor rather than a one-shot generator. The incumbent acts as a moving scaffold. Future samples are drawn near the current best program instead of restarting from the original problem statement. That creates a simple local search process in context space, not in parameter space. The paper’s main contribution is showing how far that alone can go.e:T79c,The evaluation covers three verifiable mathematical optimization problems with three open-weight models: circle packing with gpt-oss-20b, sums/differences of finite sets with OLMo-3.1-32B-Instruct, and Erdős’ minimum-overlap problem with Mistral-Small-3.1-24B-Instruct. The emphasis is practical wall-clock performance, not only sample efficiency.

The headline result is on circle packing: Hill Sampling reaches the best published result in under five hours on eight H100 GPUs. On the Erdős minimum-overlap problem, it surpasses the AlphaEvolve algorithmic-discovery reference within 12 hours. The abstract is truncated, but the body makes the qualitative outcome clear: one new published best result and strong competitive performance on the other tasks.

The ablations are the most informative part. Hill Sampling substantially outperforms repeated sampling, showing that best-so-far conditioning is the main source of gain. Standard ES often improves mean return while reducing maximum return, which directly supports the paper’s framing that mean-oriented updates can be misaligned with discovery objectives. Setting the ES learning rate to zero removes learning but keeps random weight perturbations; this sometimes preserves maximum return better than learned ES, yet still underperforms plain token-sampling randomness from the unperturbed model. That is a useful boundary condition for claims about nearby experts in pretrained weight space: even if such experts exist, searching for them may be less effective than output-space exploration when exact verification is available.

The paper also compares Hill Sampling with N=64 and N=512 under the same total sampling budget, changing the trade-off between more frequent incumbent updates and larger within-round candidate sets. Even without all table values here, the setup is worth attention because it probes whether iterative conditioning matters more than wider one-shot search.f:T514,For a quick read, focus on three parts. First, the opening motivation where the paper distinguishes maximizing best-found reward from improving average sample quality. That framing explains nearly every later result. Second, the formal definition of Hill Sampling in the methods section, especially the stored incumbent reward, equal-reward acceptance, and the budget-matched N=64 versus N=512 variants. Third, the ES and model-noise comparison, which addresses a broader question than the benchmark itself: should test-time exploration happen in token space or weight space?

If you have 20–30 minutes, read in this order: the main algorithm figure, the primary result tables for the three tasks, the ES comparison plots, then the discussion. The key is not the benchmark details but the way the authors strip a complex discovery pipeline down to one necessary mechanism.

Two points are worth deeper thought. One is whether Hill Sampling depends on the locality and compositionality of program editing; the same recipe may transfer less cleanly to natural-language proofs or unverifiable reasoning. The other is the tension between max return and mean return. That distinction has direct implications for verifier-guided decoding, best-of-N methods, and some RL-style test-time adaptation setups.10:T82e,The method, GravityOCR, is a parameter-shared AR–block-diffusion model built by adapting GLM-OCR. The same decoder is run under two attention patterns. The first path is a block-diffusion drafter. The output sequence is partitioned into contiguous blocks of size at most B. Dependencies remain causal across blocks, but attention is bidirectional within the current block. During training, a random subset of positions in the current block is masked and the model learns to denoise them conditioned on the completed prefix and the partially observed block. At inference, the current block starts fully masked and is iteratively denoised to produce a draft block in parallel.

The second path is a causal AR verifier. Given the same prefix, it checks the draft token by token and commits only the longest prefix that matches AR greedy predictions. Once a mismatch appears, commitment stops and the next drafting round begins. This design is the key idea: the diffusion path proposes, but the AR path decides. With top-1 verification, the committed output matches pure AR greedy decoding in exact arithmetic, so acceleration comes from accepting multiple drafted tokens per round rather than changing the final decision rule.

This differs from standard speculative decoding, which usually relies on a separate small drafter or an auxiliary multi-token head. Here both drafting and verification use the full shared backbone. Training combines the original AR NLL with a block-diffusion denoising objective so the model can operate under both masks. Post-training then applies GRPO only through the causal AR path using OCR rewards at the sequence and structure levels. The rationale is clean: optimize the path that determines the final output, and let shared parameters transfer improvements to the drafter indirectly. The paper also contrasts direct block-diffusion commitment with self-speculative verification. Direct commitment offers more parallelism but is brittle on tables, formulas, and other structurally coupled outputs; verification trades some parallelism for AR-like reliability.11:T69d,The model is evaluated on OmniDocBench v1.6 after adapting GLM-OCR. Four results matter most. First, the final model retains almost all of the original AR model’s quality: 95.16 Overall versus 95.48 for the base GLM-OCR, or about 99.7% retention. Second, GRPO applied only through the AR path improves the score from 94.92 to 95.16, showing that reward optimization helps the final verifier-controlled output without breaking the shared drafter. Third, with a full self-speculative implementation in SGLang, the system reaches 9.7 tokens per forward, counting both diffusion drafting and AR verification passes. This yields a 3.94× speedup in token generation and a 1.32× speedup in end-to-end page processing over causal AR decoding. The gap between those two numbers is informative: decoder token loops are only part of the total OCR latency budget. Fourth, the paper characterizes the accuracy–parallelism trade-off. Direct block diffusion is more aggressive but loses more accuracy; self-speculative verification is limited by draft–verifier agreement, and that agreement becomes the main determinant of practical speedup.

The ablations worth noting involve block size, denoising schedule, verification behavior, and task type. Plain text OCR tends to accept longer drafts because many positions are strongly image-grounded. HTML tables and LaTeX formulas are harder because local token dependencies are stronger, so the verifier truncates drafts earlier. The paper also explicitly measures agreement with AR greedy under bf16 serving kernels, which is a useful deployment detail: exact-equivalence claims can weaken once numerical precision and kernel behavior enter the picture.12:T498,For a quick read, focus on three parts. First, read the introduction and the figure illustrating failure modes of direct parallel commitment. That makes clear why strong visual grounding does not remove token-to-token dependencies. Second, read the method section defining block-causal attention and longest-consistent-prefix verification. That is where the actual novelty sits. Third, inspect the main efficiency and quality tables: 95.16 vs 95.48, 9.7 TPF, 3.94× token speedup, and 1.32× page speedup. Those numbers tell you whether the gain is algorithmic, systems-level, or both.

If you want to go deeper, read the appendix discussion on why top-1 verification recovers AR greedy outputs and how agreement is measured under bf16 kernels. Also pay attention to the decision to run GRPO only through the AR path. That creates an interesting asymmetry: post-training improves the final decision path directly, while the drafter benefits only through shared parameters. A useful open question is whether this eventually pushes the drafter toward conservative drafts that are easy to verify but leave parallelism on the table. A solid skim should take about 20–30 minutes.13:T835,技术方案分两层。第一层是 selective absorption 机制。作者给每个位置加一个二值 absorption indicator；一旦某个位置被 absorb，它的 outgoing transition rate 置零，不再参与后续 CTMC 演化。这样就把原来“所有位置持续更新”的 uniform discrete flow，改成“部分位置冻结、其余位置继续流动”的混合过程。关键约束是：对仍然 active 的位置，保持原来的 uniform-flow velocity，不去改训练目标，也不重写 denoiser。

第二层是如何决定吸收顺序。随机吸收显然不行，因为一旦把错 token 固定住，就失去后续纠错机会。作者提出 LEDFlow，用 predictive entropy 做局部可靠性估计：每一步在给定 absorption budget 下，选当前熵最低的 active positions，把它们按当前最可能 token 固定下来；其余位置继续按 kinetic-optimal velocity 更新。下一步再重新计算熵，因此顺序是自适应的，而不是预先排好的静态 order。

理论部分的重点不是证明整个 sampler 全局最优，而是分析“不可逆吸收”引入的误差。作者把 stepwise absorption error 分解成两个 KL 项：一个对应被同时吸收位置之间的 joint dependence 丢失，另一个对应在已吸收上下文条件下的 conditional prediction error。随后在 oracle-reachable states 和 entropy-error regularity 的假设下，证明在固定吸收预算时，选择最低熵位置可以最小化 conditional error 上界。这个论证的意义在于解释为什么局部熵是合理信号，而不是仅凭经验有效。

作者还专门讨论了 global entropy lookahead。直觉上，往前看几步似乎能做更优决策，但论文指出在 denoiser 不完美时，未来状态估计误差会层层累积，decision error bound 会随 lookahead window 增长。也就是说，复杂的全局规划未必比局部熵更稳，反而更贵、更脆弱。这一点和很多生成式推理系统里的经验是相通的：如果模型本身有偏差，过深的 rollout 可能放大而不是消除误差。14:T89f,The method has two layers. The first is selective absorption. Each position is assigned a binary absorption indicator; once a position is absorbed, its outgoing transition rates are set to zero, so it no longer evolves under the CTMC. This changes the original all-positions-update regime of uniform discrete flow into a mixed process where some positions are frozen and others continue to move. A key design choice is that the velocity on active positions is left unchanged, so the method does not alter the trained denoiser or the underlying flow objective.

The second layer is the absorption policy. Randomly freezing positions is risky because an incorrect absorbed token cannot be corrected later. The proposed sampler, LEDFlow, uses predictive entropy as a local reliability signal. At each step, under a fixed absorption budget, it selects the active positions with the lowest entropy and absorbs them using their current most likely values. The remaining positions continue evolving under the kinetic-optimal velocity. Entropy is recomputed at every step, so the generation order is adaptive rather than predetermined.

The theory is framed around the error introduced by irreversible absorption. The authors decompose the stepwise absorption error into two KL terms: one for the loss of joint dependence among simultaneously absorbed positions, and one for conditional prediction error under the context created by previously absorbed values. Under assumptions on oracle-reachable states and entropy-error regularity, they show that, with a fixed absorption budget, choosing the lowest-entropy positions minimizes an upper bound on the conditional error term. This does not prove global optimality, but it gives a principled reason why local entropy is the right signal.

The paper also analyzes global entropy lookahead. In principle, evaluating future successor states could yield better decisions. In practice, with an imperfect denoiser, future-state estimation errors accumulate, and the decision-error bound grows with the lookahead window. The implication is important: more global planning is not necessarily more reliable, and can be both slower and less robust than a local entropy rule.15:T61d,The experiments span three task groups: structured reasoning, multimodal understanding, and text-to-image generation. The reasoning results are the most informative because they expose the exact failure mode the method is designed to fix.

In the Sudoku trajectory diagnostic, the native kinetic-optimal sampler produces 9.4% correct-to-wrong reversions, while LEDFlow reduces this to 2.6%. The fraction of token changes occurring after t = 0.75 also drops from 46% to 15%, which is a strong mechanistic result: the method is not just improving final accuracy, it is specifically suppressing unstable late revisions.

On Nikoli Sudoku, LEDFlow reaches 0.845 solve accuracy and achieves the best average rank across nine reasoning tasks. The pattern matters more than the single number: gains are largest on tasks with strong structural dependence, where late token flips are especially costly.

For text-to-image generation, LEDFlow achieves the best GenEval overall score among decode-time samplers, with a 2.8% absolute improvement over the native sampler. For multimodal understanding, it improves over the native sampler on all six reported benchmarks. The ablations are also useful. Entropy-guided absorption outperforms alternative absorption orders, supporting the central design choice. For lookahead, mean absolute score error rises from 0.17 with a single lookahead term to 1.76 under full lookahead, while longer lookahead can cost about 8x more inference time without meaningful gains. That empirical pattern lines up well with the theoretical analysis.16:T464,This is a skim paper, but it is worth a careful read if you work on diffusion LM, discrete flow, or any-order generation. Start with the introduction, especially the discussion of terminal transition instability and correct-to-wrong revisions. Then read the selective absorption definition and the LEDFlow algorithm in Section 3 to understand exactly how active and absorbed positions are handled in the CTMC. In the theory, focus on the KL decomposition and the entropy-based upper-bound argument; it is more important to understand what is being bounded than to follow every proof detail.

In the experiments, prioritize the Sudoku trajectory analysis, the comparison across absorption orders, and the lookahead-window error/runtime study. Those sections reveal the mechanism most clearly. If you only want the main idea, 30 to 45 minutes should be enough. If you want to connect it to D3PM, SEDD, MDLM, LLaDA, or masked-LM-style commitment strategies, spend extra time on the problem formulation and sampler pseudocode and think about how confidence-based freezing might transfer to other non-AR token generation schemes.17:T699,实验覆盖六个 1.1B 到 7B 模型，来自 Llama、Qwen、Mistral、OLMoE 等四个系列，并额外在 12.2B 的 Mistral-Nemo-Instruct-2407 上刻画 divergence。任务包括 GSM8K、HumanEval、MBPP 等基准，并测试三种 GPU microarchitecture。主要结果是：BF16 与 FP16 的 greedy 生成有 49–100% 的 prompt 出现输出差异，平均长度差为 34 tokens。TinyLlama-1.1B 在三个公开基准上的 divergence 为 59–70%；Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct 达到 82%；部分 BF16-saturated Qwen 变体达到 100%。在 Qwen2.5-3B GSM8K 上，19% 的 prompt 发生最终答案正确性翻转，说明这不是只影响表述形式的差异。误差尺度上，翻转集中在 top-two margin 接近 per-logit perturbation scale 的区域，文中给出的 σz 约为 0.026。五个干预实验验证机制预测：整数化和 temperature sharpening 没有修复效果；top-K FP32 recomputation 只要 K≥2 就有效；完整 FP32 lm_head recomputation 与 top-K 接近；继续把 FP32 范围扩展到 lm_head 之外反而让 agreement 从 63% 降到 44%。相对于 FP32 greedy reference，干预能让 BF16 arm 的 sequence agreement 提升 19–21 个百分点，但如果部署格式可自由选择，FP16 本身仍比 BF16+intervention 更接近 FP32。FP8 实验显示边界条件：若只把 FP8 放在 head projection，format-rescaled gate 在 TinyLlama 上可达到精确一致，在 Qwen2.5-3B 上提升 56 个百分点；若所有 linear layer 都量化到 FP8，误差变为 body-dominated，head-scope 修复只恢复约 1 个百分点。FlashAttention-2 下干预仍有效，但 batch size 到 8 及以上时，lm_head-only 的修复范围开始失效。18:T423,The authors run controlled BF16-vs-FP16 greedy decoding experiments and then analyze the first token at which the trajectories diverge. The central condition is simple: a flip occurs when the original logit gap between the winning token and a competitor is smaller than the precision-induced directional perturbation along that pairwise decision direction. Body error accumulates through the transformer, but it is not sufficient to separate flipping from non-flipping steps. The decisive event is usually at the lm_head, where a small top-two margin meets format-dependent rounding. The paper supports this with divergence-conditioned tracing, layer grafting, KV-cache grafting, and logit-lens traceback. These analyses converge on the lm_head as the dominant amplification stage. The proposed repair is selective FP32 recomputation of the lm_head when the top-two margin falls below a threshold. This choice is more targeted than running larger parts of the model in FP32: the problematic computation is the final argmax boundary, not every layer uniformly.19:T5e3,The study covers six models from 1.1B to 7B across Llama, Qwen, Mistral, and OLMoE families, with an additional 12.2B Mistral-Nemo probe. Benchmarks include GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP, and the authors test multiple GPU microarchitectures. BF16-vs-FP16 greedy generations diverge on 49–100% of prompts, with a mean length difference of 34 tokens. TinyLlama-1.1B shows 59–70% divergence across public benchmarks. Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct reaches 82%, and some BF16-saturated Qwen variants reach 100%. On Qwen2.5-3B GSM8K, 19% of prompts flip final-answer correctness. The perturbation scale around the logits is reported as roughly σz ≈ 0.026, and flips concentrate at small top-two margins. Five interventions match the mechanism’s predictions: integer quantization and temperature sharpening do not help; top-K FP32 recomputation works for K≥2; full FP32 lm_head recomputation matches top-K; extending FP32 beyond the lm_head reduces agreement from 63% to 44%. Against an FP32 greedy reference, the intervention improves BF16 sequence agreement by 19–21 percentage points, although FP16 alone remains the closer single-format proxy. FP8 experiments define the boundary: head-only FP8 with a rescaled gate can recover exact agreement on TinyLlama and +56 points on Qwen2.5-3B, while end-to-end FP8 becomes body-dominated and gains only about +1 point from the head repair. FlashAttention-2 does not break the repair in the tested setting, but the lm_head-only scope weakens at batch size 8 and above.1a:T496,The experiment transfers state from Qwen3.5-4B-Base to Qwen3.5-9B-Base. The models differ in size and hidden width, 2,560 versus 4,096, but their Gated DeltaNet persistent-state geometry matches exactly. The state is decomposed into attention keys and values, recurrent matrices, convolution buffers, and metadata. After the source processes a 4,096-token prefix, the receiver starts from the real next token and is evaluated on the following teacher-forced targets, with no target-side replay of the historical prefix. Attention KV is translated with ridge maps per layer, head, and role; keys are de-rotated before mapping and re-rotated for the target. Recurrent and convolution states are either directly reused or mapped with learned ridge-style translators. A factorial component experiment tests direct versus translated choices for K, R, and C. The selected base configuration translates KV but directly reuses both GDN components. A small identity-anchored correction is then trained with both language models frozen, using full-vocabulary KL from native 9B behavior to the handoff behavior plus an identity penalty. The trainable correction has 434,176 parameters.1b:T87f,The proposed method, QuantWM, is a training-free 2-bit KV-cache quantization framework. Its goal is not merely to reduce Key/Value reconstruction error, but to explicitly preserve attention logits and temporal consistency. It builds on the centroid-residual representation used in QVG: each Key is assigned to a BF16 centroid, and only the residual is quantized to low bitwidth. The paper argues that QVG is limited because centroid selection is driven mainly by K-means geometry, which optimizes feature-space distance rather than downstream impact on QK^T.

The first component is QSAC, quantization-sensitivity-aware clustering. Instead of selecting centroids purely by Key-space clustering quality, QSAC jointly considers Query sensitivity and residual dynamic range, aiming to reduce the effect of INT2 residual quantization on attention logits. Intuitively, some Key directions may have small Euclidean error but still be highly dangerous if they align with Query-sensitive directions and alter token ranking. QSAC tries to avoid such residual structures already at the clustering stage.

The second component is PSAC, principal-subspace attention compensation. Even with better clustering, Key quantization error can still be amplified along dominant Query directions and distort logits. PSAC extracts the principal Query subspace and uses low-rank projections to represent and compensate the Key error components along those sensitive directions during inference. The idea is not to fully reconstruct Keys, which is unrealistic at 2 bits, but to correct the error components that matter most for attention allocation. This is a different philosophy from simply increasing quantization fidelity: it accepts that full preservation is impossible and spends the available budget on the subspace that most affects retrieval behavior.

The overall design is coherent: QSAC makes Keys more quantization-friendly before compression, and PSAC compensates the most harmful attention shifts after compression. Because the method is training-free, it is intended as a practical drop-in replacement for existing KV-cache compression pipelines in video generation and world models.1c:T53a,The experiments cover several representative models, including LingBot-World-v2, Matrix-Game-2, LongCat-Video, HY-World 1.5, and Causal-Forcing, which suggests the authors are aiming for architecture-agnostic evidence rather than a single-model result. The headline outcome is that QuantWM substantially reduces temporal flickering, blur, and artifacts under 2-bit KV quantization while maintaining strong benchmark performance on metrics such as VBench. It also achieves up to 6.20x KV-cache memory compression with limited inference overhead.

The most important evidence comes from the diagnostic ablations. The paper separates Key-only and Value-only quantization and shows that most degradation comes from Keys. It then compares reconstruction errors and finds that Keys can have smaller numerical error than Values despite causing worse output quality. That result supports the paper's central claim: KV quantization should not be judged mainly by reconstruction magnitude, but by its effect on attention logits and token ranking. The method components are built directly around that diagnosis. Even without all table values in the provided text, the experimental story is clear: the goal is not just to preserve benchmark scores, but to fix temporal-consistency failures that benchmarks under-measure but humans notice immediately.1d:T7c0,This is a measurement study, not a new agent method. The authors fix a ReAct-style coding-agent harness on a multi-tool aggregation task and evaluate protocol fidelity rather than task success. The design has three important pieces.

First, it separates channel from prompt. “Channel” means tool schemas are passed through the native tools= field and then rendered and parsed by the server. “Prompt” means the tool list and JSON call format are written directly into the textual prompt. The paper compares three settings: native, which uses tools= only; native+hint, which still uses tools= but also injects a plain-text tool list and strict JSON format instructions; and text-tools, which removes tools= entirely and parses calls from text. This isolates server-side protocol handling from the model’s ability to follow tool instructions.

Second, it refines the label space. Each assistant turn is categorized as valid in-schema call, hallucinated call, unparseable text, no-call prose, or non-response. The non-response category is crucial. In Ollama, some models trigger HTTP 400 request rejection before inference; the harness retries, gives up, and writes a generic error string into the trajectory. If that error is not preserved as structured metadata, downstream analysis can count it as a model non-call. The paper therefore excludes non-responses from the fidelity denominator and reports them separately.

Third, it performs a multi-stack comparison. The same weights and the same request behave differently under Ollama, llama.cpp, vLLM, and SGLang: some stacks reject tools=, some accept it but return calls as plain text, and some require explicit parser configuration before a call is recognized. The point is not to rank stacks, but to show that the measured outcome is stack-dependent. The constrained-decoding baseline adds another useful negative result: forcing valid call syntax can simply move the failure mode from malformed calls to endless looping.1e:T668,The experiments cover small local models including Qwen2.5-Coder 0.5B/1.5B/3B, Llama-3.2, Phi-3, and Gemma-3. Results are reported over 8 seeds using both turn-pooled rates and per-seed means, with seed-level bootstrap 95% confidence intervals. Four findings stand out. First, under Ollama’s default native function-calling path, Qwen2.5-Coder models accept tools= but return calls as text, Llama-3.2 returns native tool_calls, and Phi-3 and Gemma-3 are rejected with HTTP 400 before inference. Without structured error logging, those rejected models can be falsely reported at 0% fidelity. Second, for models whose requests are accepted, native+hint often recovers much of the fidelity, which suggests that some failures come from protocol rendering rather than inability to use tools. But Llama-3.2 degrades under a uniform text-only protocol, so the best setup is model-dependent. Third, the same weights behave differently across four serving stacks: models rejected by Ollama can run on llama.cpp, vLLM rejects similar requests by default, and SGLang accepts them but returns text calls. That directly shows the benchmark outcome is partly a serving-stack property. Fourth, turn-pooled and per-seed metrics can differ by roughly 55 points because a single looping episode can dominate the pooled denominator. This makes aggregation choice a major source of instability.

The paper also includes a useful negative result: at this measurement precision, the authors do not support broader claims about scale, model family, or reasoning ability and explicitly avoid making them. That restraint is one of the stronger parts of the study.1f:T418,For a skim, start with the introduction and the method section explaining the OpenAI-compatible tool-calling pipeline. The key point is that tools= is not passed directly to the model; it is mediated by server-side template rendering and output parsing. Then read the comparison among native, native+hint, and text-tools, plus the four-stack behavior table or figure. Those sections make the confound concrete. Finally, look at how non-response is defined and removed from the fidelity denominator. That detail determines whether a reported 0% is meaningful or simply an artifact.

If you only have 20 minutes, that is enough to get the main lesson. If you are building your own evaluation stack, the practical checklist is worth extracting: log HTTP and serving failures explicitly, separate request rejection from model non-calls, report both pooled and per-seed metrics, and document parser/template configuration. It is not a methods paper, but it is a useful guardrail for anyone evaluating local agents or constructing tool-use training data.20:T445,The experiments run on L40S, A100, and H100/H200-class settings as described in the supplied text, using 8B to 70B base models across three model families. Four frontier agents are evaluated: Claude Opus 5, GPT-5.6-luna, Gemini 3.7 Flash, and DeepSeek V4-Pro.

In 38 certified real episodes, the agents produce 0 TBDL deliveries and 0 gate bypasses. The 12 end-to-end TBDL runs observed arise from an excluded contract. The delivery contract rejects 31 out of 38 metric-passing deliveries, mainly due to requirements such as schema-checked model cards, reconciled cost reports, and registry CI. Providing the oracle-correct configuration fixes acceptance in 24/24 cases, but 22/24 are still rejected, indicating that many failures are governance and artifact-quality failures rather than pure training-configuration errors. Under pressure tests, the three flagship agents preserve the governance gate in 360/360 deliverable trials and refuse 60/60 infeasible tickets. Human FDE plus assistant baselines have judgment residuals of 0.06 and 0.09, while the closest autonomous agent reaches 0.10.21:T886,The method has a data component and a training component. For data, Video-HopChain does not simply concatenate single-hop video questions. Each example is a dependency chain of 3 to 6 hops. Every hop is a yes/no question about one or two moments in the same video, and each answer maps to one of two integers. The final answer is the sum across hops, which makes the task exactly verifiable by exact match. Two design choices matter. First, every question includes at least one temporal-order hop, so a single frame cannot contain all required evidence. Second, some hops are selectors: the answer to an earlier hop determines which later moment should be inspected next, creating real inter-hop dependence rather than a bag of independent subquestions. The generation pipeline first produces shot-level captions with a VLM, then uses a text-only LLM to synthesize the multi-hop question from those captions. This is a deliberate trade-off: direct end-to-end VLM question generation is harder to control and harder to verify, while operating in caption space gives tighter control over logic structure and answer form.

For training, the paper introduces Confidence-Gated Exploration on top of GRPO. Standard GRPO derives its signal from reward variance within a group of rollouts for the same prompt. If all rewards in the group are identical, the group yields no relative advantage. CGE addresses this without drawing extra rollouts. With group size G=8, the first 4 rollouts are sampled normally. If those 4 are all correct or all incorrect, the remaining 4 are sampled with a top-token mask inside the reasoning span. Whenever the model assigns more than tau=0.95 probability mass to a single token, that token is temporarily removed and sampling is renormalized over the rest. This is not generic noise injection; it is targeted exploration only when the model is highly confident and the group is likely to collapse. Masked positions are excluded from the token-level loss, but all 8 rollouts still participate in the group advantage computation. That avoids the extra rollout cost of resampling while also avoiding direct supervision on tokens that were explicitly perturbed.22:T512,The dataset contains 22,550 training questions over 13,378 videos, plus a held-out benchmark of 1,000 questions on 1,000 videos. The training target is Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, further optimized with GRPO and evaluated on 8 general-purpose video benchmarks. The base model averages 55.4 across the 8 benchmarks. Training on Video-HopChain raises this to 57.9, a gain of 2.5 points, and all 8 benchmarks improve rather than only a subset. That matters more than a single benchmark gain because it suggests the synthesized multi-hop supervision is not merely overfitting to its own task format. Adding CGE on top further raises the average to 59.3, another +1.4 over Video-HopChain alone and +3.9 over the original baseline.

The experimental narrative also ties CGE gains specifically to recovering collapsed groups rather than simply increasing randomness. Another useful aspect is that the paper emphasizes transfer to general video understanding benchmarks, not only performance on its own benchmark. That makes it more relevant as a training-signal paper than as a pure dataset release. The limitations are also clear: question generation is still bottlenecked by shot-caption quality, and the reward remains final-answer exact match, with no finer-grained credit assignment over intermediate hops.23:T57b,The key idea is to turn long-horizon decision quality into a binary choice at a decision fork. The authors observe that in software engineering and machine-learning research tasks, agents often make multiple attempts at the same task, or they take a detour and later correct themselves within a single run. If two trajectories share an equivalent prefix and then diverge, and if later evidence clearly shows which branch was better, that divergence can be converted into a taste question. Each question contains the task, the trajectory prefix up to the fork, and two candidate directions. The model must choose the better direction without seeing the later outcome. The benchmark is built from two mining procedures: parallel trajectories, where independent attempts diverge on the same task, and detour trajectories, where a single run reveals a wrong turn followed by correction. To reduce label noise, the authors do not simply use final success as supervision. They keep forks where the outcome difference can be credibly attributed to the judgment at the fork, using later evidence such as test results or research scores. On top of the benchmark, they introduce a training setup where a teacher model, given knowledge of the correct branch, produces reasoning that is then distilled into a student. This turns hindsight into a trainable judgment policy rather than a static benchmark-only signal.24:T437,The paper releases Taste-Bench, a benchmark of 502 taste questions spanning software engineering and machine-learning research tasks. The first set of experiments validates the benchmark itself: questions become harder as the horizon from the fork to the eventual outcome increases, which supports the claim that the benchmark measures long-horizon judgment rather than shallow pattern matching. The authors also perform human review and report close agreement between mined labels and human judgments when reviewers are allowed to inspect the recorded outcomes, which supports label quality. Model evaluations show that current systems still struggle on taste, and strong end-to-end performance does not automatically imply strong taste. The most important result is that taste appears trainable: distilling teacher reasoning conditioned on the correct direction improves fork judgments on unseen tasks and also leads to end-to-end gains on held-out agent tasks. The provided text does not include the full numeric tables, so exact accuracies or gains should not be quoted here.25:T43c,This paper deserves more than a quick skim if you care about agent training signals. Start with the introduction to separate taste from ordinary task success. Then read the benchmark construction carefully, especially the definitions of parallel forks and detour forks, the filtering criteria, and the source of labels. That section determines whether the benchmark is actually measuring strategic judgment. Next, inspect the human validation and the horizon-based difficulty analysis. If your interest is training rather than evaluation, spend time on the distillation section: the move from hindsight evidence to reusable reasoning is the most transferable idea in the paper. In the experiments, prioritize benchmark statistics, performance by horizon length, and the before/after results of taste distillation on held-out tasks. A focused read should take about 45 to 60 minutes. The two main questions to keep in mind are whether execution quality still leaks into the labels, and whether this hindsight supervision can scale into more general agent RL or process-reward modeling.26:T43b,The evaluation covers preference ranking, evidence-grounded factuality, final-answer adjudication, and simple style or format checks. The data include 400 RewardBench pairs, 350 JudgeBench GPT-4o pairs, 240 HaluEval evidence-grounded judgments, 150 saved multi-turn answer-consistency replies, 108 GSM8K challenge-conversation final replies, and 64 synthetic evidence judgments, for 1,312 items in total. A 642-item pilot was frozen before inference and split 40/60 into selection and test subsets; a 670-item extension was frozen before inspecting pilot accuracy. JEV comes within about three percentage points of the strongest LLM comparator on ordinary preference and evidence-grounded factuality while costing about 0.36% of that comparator’s measured fee. Larger gaps appear on tasks that require checking derivations or resisting fluent but incorrect answers. Confidence analysis shows that many of JEV’s misses are concentrated in low-confidence regions, which makes selective escalation viable, but the paper also reports failures of threshold transfer on some workloads.27:T6ff,The paper is strongest in its controlled experimental design. Instead of making a generic complaint that compile rate is imperfect, it isolates five distinct failure modes of a metric. First, sensitivity to real improvement: if an intervention materially improves generated code, does compile rate move accordingly? Second, confounding by measurement setup: how much of the failure comes from the model versus the isolated-function harness, stub injection, syntax-only compilation, and dataset preprocessing artifacts? Third, toolchain dependence: does the same patch receive different judgments under different compilation configurations? Fourth, disagreement with an independent signal: does compile rate align with other indicators of repair quality? Fifth, gameability: if one optimizes toward compilation, does the system drift toward degenerate behaviors such as code deletion, conservative copying, or syntax-preserving but semantically useless edits?

The paper then studies the common similarity fallback, whole-function CodeBLEU, and shows why it fails structurally in this setting. Real fixes often modify only a small span, while most of the function remains unchanged. A metric computed over the whole function therefore gives large credit for preserved context, even when the vulnerability is untouched. To mitigate this, the authors examine diff_F1 as a change-aware screen rather than a repair-quality metric. It scores overlap only in the edited region, so a no-op copy gets zero credit, and some compile-oriented deletion patches receive near-zero credit as well. The authors are careful not to oversell it: diff_F1 can still over-credit some deletion-style patches, so it should be used as a filter before deeper analysis, not as a substitute for tests or exploit oracles.28:T68e,The empirical setup is reasonably substantial for this niche: 203 vulnerable functions from Big-Vul, spanning 7 CWEs; 3 open-source code LLMs ranging from 350M to 6.7B parameters; 3 prompting strategies; and 5 samples per setting, with bootstrap confidence intervals. Several results stand out.

First, compile rate is weakly responsive to interventions that materially improve generated code. The paper’s point is not just that the metric is noisy, but that it can miss genuine quality changes.

Second, roughly 64% of compile failures are not attributable to the model. They come from missing project context and a specific Big-Vul preprocessing artifact, and this share is nearly invariant across model sizes. This is the central quantitative result because it shows that the dominant source of failure is the evaluation setup, not the model.

Third, whole-function CodeBLEU is shown to be badly misleading: a no-op baseline that simply copies the vulnerable input scores higher than every tested model. That sharply limits its interpretability in single-function repair.

Fourth, diff_F1 behaves better on several observed gaming patterns. It gives zero credit to no-ops, near-zero credit to some deletion patches that compile by removing problematic code, and nonzero credit to edits overlapping the developer’s actual change. The paper also documents where this screen still fails.

Finally, the benchmark-scale comparison itself is useful. Even though the paper is not primarily a model leaderboard, running three models and three prompting strategies over 203 functions is enough to expose confidence intervals and metric instability that smaller studies often hide.29:Tac6,系统围绕 Listen–Think–Act–Speak 循环设计。推理时包含两条 Audio Encoder + LLM 路径：一条用于 full-duplex 决策，判断继续听、开始说、停止或恢复；另一条用于 speech-to-text 风格的响应内容生成。随后 context-aware voice renderer 根据文本内容、对话历史、声音线索和声学上下文生成流式语音。这个拆分的取舍是清楚的：内容生成、交互控制和语音渲染承担不同约束，避免把所有行为压到单一解码器里。

Think 层是 foundation post-training。流程包含四步：Core-Cocktail 冷启动、Multimodality OPD、domain GRPO specialists、Multi-Teacher OPD consolidation。Core-Cocktail 从 audio-pretrained AudioLM 出发，用 source Text LLM 作为语言参数锚点，先对齐共享语言参数，再用受控 SFT 建立可用的音频策略。训练数据约一百万小时 paired audio-text，覆盖通用音频理解、语音指令、多轮交互、工具请求、情绪与共情、语用意图、声学场景理解等。

M2-OPD 是关键。Audio Student 先在音频输入上采样自己的轨迹；Text Teacher 接收语义等价的文本提示和同一学生前缀，给出每个 token 位置的分布；Frozen Audio Reference 接收原始音频上下文和同一前缀，也给出分布。也就是说，教师不是提前生成答案让学生模仿，而是在学生的 on-policy 轨迹上逐步评分。这能减少离线蒸馏中的分布错位：学生在自己会访问的状态上学习，同时利用 Text LLM 的语言推理能力和 Audio Reference 的音频条件约束。

Act 层是方法中心。论文构造 executable environments，环境中包含工具、策略、数据库和任务。环境会经过验证，并通过 rollout 结果持续演化。训练不只看函数调用语法，而是看工具选择、状态校验、长期任务是否完成、是否该拒绝、进度沟通是否有根据。优化使用 GRPO，并把反馈分成 dialogue-level、milestone-level、turn-level。dialogue-level 约束整体任务完成，milestone-level 给长程过程提供中间信号，turn-level 处理局部响应和工具反馈解释。这种多粒度奖励比只给最终成功/失败更适合语音交互，因为用户中途改约束、打断、等待都会改变最优行为。

Speak and Coordinate 层把“说什么”之外的行为纳入策略：何时说、是否该说、何时行动、何时等待。它处理 full-duplex interaction 中常见的冲突，例如用户未说完时不应抢话，工具运行时不应给出超过证据的结论，用户修改目标时需要中止或更新执行计划。论文还提出单独的 Voice Harness，用显式任务边界和记忆边界约束评测与执行。2a:Tb11,The system is organized around a Listen–Think–Act–Speak loop. At inference time, two Audio Encoder + LLM paths are used. One path makes full-duplex decisions such as keep listening, start speaking, stop, or resume. The other generates response content as text. A context-aware voice renderer then uses the content, dialogue history, voice cues, and acoustic context to produce streaming speech. This separation makes the control problem cleaner: response content, turn-taking policy, and voice rendering have different constraints.

The Think layer uses a four-stage foundation post-training pipeline: Core-Cocktail cold start, Multimodality OPD, domain GRPO specialists, and Multi-Teacher OPD consolidation. Core-Cocktail starts from an audio-pretrained AudioLM and uses the source Text LLM as an anchor for shared language parameters. It then applies controlled SFT over audio and text instruction data. The foundation data scale is about one million hours of paired audio-text data, covering general audio understanding, spoken instruction following, multi-turn interaction, tool-oriented requests, emotion, empathy, pragmatic intent, and acoustic-scene understanding.

M2-OPD is the core capability-transfer mechanism. The Audio Student samples a response trajectory conditioned on audio. At each token position, the Text Teacher receives a semantically equivalent text prompt plus the same student prefix, while the frozen Audio Reference receives the original audio context plus the same prefix. Both provide token-level distributions on the student’s on-policy trajectory. The teacher does not generate a separate answer for imitation; it scores the states actually visited by the student. This design combines text-grounded reasoning supervision with audio-conditioned constraints.

The Act layer builds executable environments that include tools, policies, databases, and tasks. These environments are validated and evolved using rollout feedback. Training uses GRPO with dialogue-level, milestone-level, and turn-level rollouts. Dialogue-level rewards capture final task success, milestone-level rewards provide intermediate signals for long-horizon execution, and turn-level rewards handle local behavior such as tool-feedback interpretation or progress communication. This is a stronger formulation than function-call SFT because the target behavior includes execution state, policy constraints, refusal, and evidence-bounded communication.

The Speak and Coordinate layer learns a conversational policy over how, when, and whether to speak or act. It handles cases such as user interruption, waiting until the user finishes, changing the target while a query is running, or withholding unsupported conclusions. The separate Voice Harness introduces explicit task and memory boundaries for evaluation and control.2b:T4f5,The concrete scale reported in the provided material is approximately one million hours of paired audio-text data for the foundation recipe. Parameter count, tokenizer details, total training tokens, and full benchmark tables are not included in the supplied content.

The evaluation is organized into intelligence, action, interaction, and real-time dimensions, comparing Qwen-Audio-3.0-Realtime with Qwen-Audio-3.1-Realtime. Intelligence covers audio reasoning, multilingual understanding, and multi-turn instruction following. Action covers tool use, feedback interpretation, task completion, refusal, and grounded progress reporting. Interaction covers conversational behavior and full-duplex decisions. Real-time evaluation covers streaming usability and latency-related behavior.

The most informative ablations to look for are: removing the Text Teacher in OPD to measure loss of text-grounded reasoning transfer; removing the Audio Reference to test audio-conditioned drift; replacing multi-granularity GRPO with final-only rewards to test long-horizon tool execution; and removing Speak and Coordinate to measure turn-taking and unsupported-progress errors. The supplied text describes the training design but does not expose the full numeric ablation results.2c:T407,OmniFysics-Nano-V2 is a compact 4B omni-modal model that accepts image, video, audio, speech, and text inputs, and can generate text and speech. The data pipeline has two physics-aware branches. The static branch links salient objects to a structured physical prototype bank, adds intrinsic-property annotations, and enforces plausible value ranges and cross-property compatibility. The dynamic branch builds event-centered video examples by aligning visible motion, state transitions, and acoustic transients, so that an interaction is represented from its initial state through intermediate responses to the final outcome. The training pipeline then applies a policy-aware curriculum. For each candidate prompt, four stochastic rollouts are sampled; only prompts with reward variation are retained, since they provide a meaningful within-group learning signal. GRPO training is organized into two stages: the first improves general answer accuracy, and the second strengthens physical understanding using format-specific rewards.2d:T85f,The paper organizes multimodal LMs into three families: encoder-full, encoder-free, and discrete-token models. Encoder-full systems use a dedicated perceptual encoder to produce continuous semantic features before the shared transformer. Encoder-free and discrete-token systems do not. The central hypothesis is that the missing perceptual computation is internalized by the front part of the shared transformer.

To test this, the authors use three complementary analyses. First, layerwise linear probing asks when modality-token hidden states become linearly decodable for concept identity. In encoder-full models, decodable semantics should already be present at the transformer input. In encoder-free and discrete-token models, semantic organization should emerge progressively with depth. Second, representational similarity analysis compares intermediate states with dedicated perceptual encoders to see whether early and middle layers exhibit encoder-like geometry. Third, causal intervention perturbs image and audio inputs and measures at what depths modality-token states remain causally necessary for the final output, which helps identify where the representation is formed and where it is read out.

Gemma 4 12B is the main target model. Cross-model comparisons include encoder-free EVE and Fuyu, discrete-token Chameleon, and encoder-full controls such as LLaVA-1.5, Qwen3-VL, Qwen3-Omni, and Qwen2-Audio. For aligned image-audio analysis, the paper builds a paired concept dataset from ImageNet-1k and ESC-50 with 31 shared concepts, 40 images and 40 audio clips per concept, for 1,240 samples per modality.

The key claim is not just that some layer performs well. It is that there exists a contiguous band of layers that transforms low-level perceptual structure into a readable semantic representation. That band is the Virtual Encoder. The paper also argues that post-readout routing differs by modality: in Gemma 4, vision briefly aligns with the language subspace near readout, while audio follows a different trajectory, suggesting that shared transformers do not collapse all modalities into one uniform internal stream.2e:T62b,There are four main findings. First, linear probing shows that concept decodability in encoder-free and discrete-token models rises progressively with depth, whereas encoder-full models enter the shared transformer with already organized perceptual representations. This supports the idea that perceptual encoding has been absorbed into the early transformer layers. Second, representational similarity analysis shows that early-to-middle hidden states in models such as Gemma 4 12B resemble dedicated perceptual encoders geometrically, indicating that these layers are not merely doing generic language processing. Third, causal analysis identifies the depth at which modality representations are read out: before a certain layer band, perturbing modality tokens strongly affects the output; after that point, the effect changes, suggesting that the encoded representation has already been handed off to later computation. Fourth, routing is modality-dependent. In Gemma 4, vision transiently approaches the language subspace near readout, while audio follows a markedly different path, implying asymmetric integration mechanisms across modalities.

The paper is more about trends and internal structure than benchmark scores. The paired dataset is also relatively small, with 31 shared concepts and 1,240 samples per modality, so the conclusions should be read as mechanistic evidence rather than a broad statistical law over open-domain semantics. Its strength comes from triangulation across probing, similarity, and causal intervention, plus clean architectural comparisons.2f:T400,The disclosed system is described through interfaces and product behavior. Gemini 3.8 Flash TTS is positioned for high-control voice design and character creation. Gemini 3.8 Flash-Lite TTS is positioned for high-volume generation, dubbing, content creation, and expressive voice agents. The models support natural-language creation of custom voices across more than 100 languages and dialects, access to a library of 2,000+ production-ready voices, reference-based voice replication from a 30-second audio sample, persistent saved voices to reduce drift across projects, and upcoming voice remixing over timbre, pitch, pace, and accent. Prompts come from the English subset of DAPO-Math-17K; after filtering, 14,107 prompts remain, and Full uses 14,080 distinct prompts. Training uses FP32, AdamW, learning rate 1e-6, 128 trajectories per update, one rollout per prompt occurrence, and a 16,384-token response limit. Evaluation is on AIME24, AIME25, and AMC23. The main result is a sign flip in the value of prompt breadth. Moving from 8 prompts to Full changes Average Accuracy by -2.10 points with frozen initial responses, +0.41 points with periodic refresh, and +1.96 points with per-update refresh. The endpoint interaction is 4.07 points with a question-paired 95% interval of [2.00, 6.28], and all three benchmarks show the same interaction sign. With ten snapshots, 8 prompts reach 24.09% average accuracy, close to 24.51% for 14,080 distinct prompts. With frozen responses, increasing breadth drops accuracy from 21.16% to 19.05%; with per-update refresh, it raises accuracy from 23.61% to 25.57%. A second matched experiment fixes 48 prompts, 4,800 trajectories, and 40 updates under two teachers, Qwen3-4B-Instruct and JustRL-DeepSeek-1.5B. Periodic generation improves solved-question coverage among 32 samples by 12.69 and 22.57 points at a 16K limit. At a larger 32K budget, frozen-response models can regain the accuracy lead, but at higher token cost.32:T4f9,The work has two parts. First, it re-evaluates prior GuidedSampling. The authors use Qwen2.5-Instruct models at 1.5B, 3B, 7B, 14B, and 32B as both concept generators and answer generators, covering all 25 size pairs. They keep the original iterative concept-generation procedure and prompts, but change the answer-generator sampling parameters. With temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95, and top-k disabled, concept guidance no longer improves over repeated sampling on MATH500.

The second part turns concept generation into a learned search policy. Instead of generating concepts one by one, the model emits many detailed, problem-specific concepts in a single autoregressive trajectory. These concepts are then used to condition the frozen answer generator, with the answer budget allocated across them. The concept generator is trained with reinforcement learning: the sampled concept trajectory is the action, and the reward is the downstream success of the answer generator. In the main setup, a Qwen2.5-7B concept generator is trained to steer a frozen Qwen2.5-32B answer generator. The key design choice is that the small model is not optimized to solve the problem directly; it is optimized to propose semantic directions that make the larger model more likely to solve it.33:T48c,The baseline study on MATH500 uses 100 answer rollouts per problem and reports pass@50. Repeated sampling spends all 100 rollouts directly from the answer generator, while concept-guided sampling uses the same answer-generation budget split across parsed concepts. Under more exploratory decoding, the previously reported gains of GuidedSampling largely disappear and the method often slightly underperforms repeated sampling.

The main result is on 1,000 held-out hard DeepMath problems. At the same answer-generation allocation, naive repeated sampling with the frozen Qwen2.5-32B answer generator reaches 19.0% pass@128. The RL-trained Qwen2.5-7B concept generator raises this to 39.2%. The trained 7B generator also outperforms an untuned 32B concept generator, suggesting the gain is not just model scale. It transfers without retraining to a Llama-3.3-70B answer generator from a different family, and the improvement persists on Omni-MATH 2. Additional analyses study answer leakage, concept relevance, position effects, and the number of generated concepts to test whether the learned policy improves exploration rather than merely embedding final answers.34:T833,The setup is a controlled intervention on teacher-only context. The training data consists of problem-solution pairs. The student always sees only the problem x. The teacher is a frozen copy of the student’s initial checkpoint and provides next-token distributions on the student’s own sampled prefixes. The only thing that changes across runs is the privileged context policy c_l(x) supplied to the teacher.

From the same problem-solution pairs, the authors compile several context levels offline. L1 is the full solution. L2 is a named strategy, giving a method label or strategy cue. L3 is a method-independent framing, which keeps the structural perspective but avoids committing to a specific derivation. L4 is a problem category, an even higher-level abstraction. L5 is answer-only, preserving the destination but removing the path. There is also a control condition that keeps only a fixed 10% prefix of the original solution plus the answer, which helps separate semantic abstraction from simple context shortening. Prompt templates and bridge wording are controlled, and within each model scale the student prompt, rollout procedure, loss, teacher checkpoint, and training set are held fixed.

Two methodological choices are especially important. First, the paper defines context utility by post-training student performance, not by a static property of the teacher distribution. That is the right target for this problem. Second, initial teacher-student KL is treated only as a diagnostic. This allows the authors to test a common intuition—that larger distributional displacement means better teaching—and show that it fails here.

Mechanistically, the difference is easy to state. A full solution gives the teacher one concrete derivation, so when the student deviates from that path the teacher may push probability mass back toward the reference trajectory. Abstract context keeps information about what kind of solution is appropriate while relaxing how it should be reached. In on-policy distillation, that changes the shape of the supervision signal, not just its strength.35:T79e,The main experiments are on competition mathematics. Students are Qwen3 models at 1.7B, 4B, and 8B. Evaluation includes three in-domain math benchmarks and four transfer benchmarks. Within each scale, the training pipeline is fixed and only the privileged context type changes. Results are reported under multiple rules, including peak mean and endpoint-style summaries, to avoid overfitting conclusions to a single checkpoint selection scheme.

There are three main findings. First, concise intermediate abstractions can outperform full solutions. In the primary runs, the best intermediate context improves in-domain peak mean over the full-solution condition by 1.4 points at 4B and 1.6 points at 8B, while using roughly an order of magnitude fewer hint tokens. Across three seeds, L3 and L4 show positive mean gains over L1 at 4B and 8B under all four reporting rules. Second, answer-only context is surprisingly competitive at larger scales: it comes within 0.2 points of the full-solution condition at 4B and 8B, but trails by 1.76 points at 1.7B. This suggests that as the student becomes stronger, preserving the destination may matter more than exposing a full path. Third, initial KL does not rank teaching utility. Full solutions induce the largest initial teacher-student KL at every scale, yet they do not produce the best trained students at 4B or 8B. Item-level analysis further shows that context changes which problems the student ends up solving, not just the overall average.

A useful robustness check recompiles the abstract contexts with Qwen3-8B and still preserves the peak advantage at larger scales, which argues against the effect being an artifact of one specific offline compiler. The supplementary studies on heterogeneous assignment and learned routing are secondary; the main result already stands: there is no universally best privileged context, and the preferred abstraction level depends on student scale and task.36:T425,This is a good skim paper, but the framing matters. Start with the introduction, especially the argument that privileged context is part of the supervision mechanism rather than auxiliary metadata. Then read the context taxonomy carefully so that L1 through L5 are clear. After that, go straight to the main result table, focusing on the 4B and 8B comparisons between L3/L4 and L1, and on the answer-only condition staying nearly tied with full solutions. Finish with the KL diagnostic section, which directly challenges the idea that larger teacher-student divergence automatically means better distillation.

If you have about 25 minutes, read in this order: setup figure, context construction section, main table, multi-seed results, KL analysis. The right lens is not “math reasoning method” but “training signal design.” Two questions are worth deeper follow-up: whether the gains come mainly from reducing reference-path bias, and whether the same pattern transfers to reasoning SFT, verifier-guided self-training, or RL-style reasoning pipelines.37:T688,The formulation represents autoregressive generation and agentic interaction through a filtration. At each step the model emits a token and may receive an environment observation; the terminal reward is a function of the complete trajectory. The conditional reward prediction is defined as V_i = E[R | F_i]. The paper then imposes three regularity conditions on token credit: Completeness, which requires total credit to explain the deviation of the final reward from its initial prediction; Prefix Consistency, which prevents future tokens from rewriting the accumulated credit of an already generated prefix; and Neutrality, which requires no systematic over- or under-crediting from the current information state. These conditions yield a unique representation, essentially the martingale difference V_i - V_{i-1}. The same construction can be aggregated to coarser units such as turns. The paper uses this representation to reinterpret OPD, RLOO, and GAE-style actor-critic methods. Under an ideal teacher, OPD is equivalent in expectation, up to scale, to the policy-gradient update induced by the unique credit. RLOO uses a response-level signal, but its expected policy-gradient contribution matches that of the token-level credit. Under bounded rewards, token-level credit is approximately sparse, which helps explain the sensitivity of GAE-based actor-critic methods in long trajectories. The proposed algorithm, PACT, uses an Actor-then-Critic update order so that critic training can apply importance sampling correction toward the updated policy. It also trains the critic with BCE rather than MSE, which is better matched to binary or bounded outcome rewards.38:T58b,The experiments cover Gemma-2-9B-IT, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K, MMLU, and BIG-Bench Hard. On Gemma, the main analysis uses 21,238 continuations. Under identical text perturbations, error propagation rises from 22% on MMLU to 41% on BBH. Within the 29 MMLU subjects, propagation varies by about 7x under a fixed format. A matched 2x2 design separates perturbation type from difficulty: text perturbations on GSM8K propagate only 6.5% of the time, while numerical perturbations on BBH multistep arithmetic reach 64.5%. On the combined 28,584-example frame, sequential variance partitioning attributes 98.8% of explained deviance to task difficulty and only 0.8% to perturbation type. The gradient replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B behaves differently: reasoning training appears to induce self-verification, reducing error propagation and shifting some cases toward self-correction under the production judge prompt. Hidden-state probes can predict behavioral type above baseline across Gemma’s 42 layers, but label choice is a major source of variance: switching from rule-based to LLM-judge labels changes 3-way accuracy by about 25 percentage points. Additive steering over 11,556 single-direction generations does not reliably flip behavior; an eight-direction extension partially controls Type C at the strongest setting, with roughly 25% flips.39:T419,The study uses Tiny Recursive Models and runs the same learned transition function beyond the nominal inference budget. The models are normally evaluated at 16 recurrent steps, but the recurrence is extended unchanged to 512 steps. Instead of comparing all examples at the same absolute recurrent step, the analysis aligns each trajectory by its first exact-solve step. This separates examples into Early solvers, Late solvers, and Persistent puzzles. The paper then measures three quantities: completion time, latent-state motion between consecutive recurrent states, and local stability. Stability is evaluated through local Jacobians and controlled finite perturbations. A key distinction is made between perturbations along the model’s natural direction of motion and perturbations along the most expanding Jacobian direction. The resulting phenomenon is trajectory-conditioned anisotropic stability: after completion, the model is contractive along its own computational path while the local recurrent map still contains expanding directions.3a:T5b8,The main experiments use 1,000 hard Sudoku puzzles and two TRM variants. Attention-A is a 6.83M-parameter recurrent transformer checkpoint. The MLP checkpoint replaces attention with sequence-axis MLP mixing and removes positional encoding. Both have a nominal budget of 16 recurrent steps. Extending recurrence to 512 steps raises cumulative exact-solve accuracy from 59.2% to 87.5% for the attention model and from 74.4% to 91.9% for the MLP model. Among puzzles unsolved at step 16, 69.4% for the attention model and 68.4% for the MLP model are solved later, meaning that more than two thirds of nominal-budget failures are unfinished computations. When aligned by first-solve time, Early and Late solvers show similar behavior near completion: latent motion drops sharply and the natural computation direction becomes locally contractive. Persistent trajectories remain more active and have a different stability profile. Perturbation experiments confirm that the most expanding Jacobian direction grows much more than same-sized perturbations along the natural motion direction. After completion, the attention model can absorb perturbations along the most expanding direction within 16 steps, while the MLP model tends to preserve them longer. Additional checks on another attention checkpoint, Easy Sudoku, and 30×30 Maze-Hard support the same qualitative picture and also show that exact-match metrics can undercount valid solutions in path-finding tasks.3b:T83a,The framework is organized around five roles. The God of Concepts provides high-level intent in text or images. The Creator, implemented with an LLM, recursively expands that intent into a world blueprint with themes, shared rules, hidden states, and system constraints. Executors are coding agents that translate the blueprint into executable entities, behaviors, and subsystems. Artists are video generation models that render observations from visual conditions derived from the executable world. Travelers act inside the world and receive observations from their own viewpoints.

Two design ideas carry most of the technical weight. The first is Logical Spaces. A world is decomposed into heterogeneous subsystems such as transportation, construction, resources, and social relations. Each subsystem has local mechanisms, but all must obey shared global constraints. Logical Spaces provides a modular structure for collaborative world construction, so different agents can implement different subsystems while remaining consistent through shared interfaces and rules. This is more controllable than asking one model to generate an entire world monolithically.

The second is Observation as World Registration. Generated observations are not treated as disposable outputs. They are registered back into a persistent world representation, so information obtained from one viewpoint or one interaction can be retained and reused later. This directly targets a weakness of standard video world models, where past frames are consumed as context but are not written back into a structured memory.

The division of labor is deliberate. CoDeR does not ask the diffusion video model to infer rules, track hidden variables, or maintain long-term state, because those capabilities are hard to learn reliably from pixel supervision alone. Instead, the LLM-plus-code layer handles explicit world logic, and the video model focuses on conditional rendering. Compared with end-to-end visual world models, this sacrifices some architectural purity but gains interpretability, editability, and long-horizon consistency.3c:T5ad,The experimental story is organized around four capabilities: persistent memory, open-ended interaction, autonomous world evolution, and multi-agent collaboration. The paper claims clear gains over prior video-based world models on these dimensions, especially for long-term consistency, off-screen state continuation, cross-view information reuse, and sustained interaction among multiple entities. From the visible content, the evaluation appears more system- and capability-oriented than a standard pretraining paper with large-scale scaling curves or tightly standardized benchmarks.

The most informative comparisons are likely to be: direct text-to-video or video continuation baselines, variants without explicit world registration, variants without Logical Spaces, and long-horizon multi-agent scenarios where hidden state matters. For this kind of system, the key question is not a single visual quality metric. It is whether explicit hidden state, modular executable structure, and observation write-back actually change the capability boundary.

For a pretraining-oriented reader, the most useful evidence would be ablations showing separate contributions from explicit hidden variables, modular logical decomposition, and observation registration. If the paper includes failure analysis, it is worth checking whether failures come from rule compilation, state drift in the execution layer, or weak condition following in the rendering layer.3d:T540,MaP has two main components. The first is Masked Trajectory Prediction. A GUI interaction is represented as a multimodal trajectory consisting of the user instruction plus, at each step, a screenshot m_t, a reasoning trace r_t, and an action a_t. Instead of defining separate input-output mappings for different tasks, the method randomly masks a fixed proportion of components in the full trajectory and trains the model to autoregressively recover the missing parts. This turns step-wise decision, state-action alignment, and long-horizon planning into the same problem: predict missing pieces given a partially observed trajectory. The important point is not masking by itself, but the move from task-specific marginal dependencies to modeling a more unified trajectory structure. The second component is role-aware adapter learning. The authors argue that heterogeneity is not only dataset-level but also token-role-level: instruction tokens, action tokens, reasoning text, and visual-state-related tokens induce different optimization directions. They therefore add token-wise adapters routed by semantic role, so different token types can use specialized representation subspaces while still sharing the backbone. Compared with dataset-specific adapters, this is a finer-grained way to handle heterogeneous supervision inside each sample.3e:T42d,The evaluation spans five GUI navigation benchmarks: AndroidWorld, AndroidControl, GUI-Odyssey, AITZ, and Mind2Web. The main comparisons are against single-task training, direct mixture training, and MaP. The qualitative result is consistent: direct mixture yields only marginal improvements, while MaP outperforms both pairwise combinations and naive joint training across benchmarks. Two experimental messages matter. First, the masked trajectory formulation itself provides a large part of the gain, suggesting that objective unification matters more than simply adding more data. Second, the role-aware adapter gives additional improvements under cross-corpus training, supporting the claim that heterogeneity lives at the token-role level rather than being only a domain shift issue. The full numeric tables are not available in the provided text, so it would be unsafe to quote exact margins.
